</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Alexander_Tilloch_Galt" title="Alexander Tilloch Galt">Alexander Tilloch Galt</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Succeeded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Donald_Smith,_1st_Baron_Strathcona_and_Mount_Royal" title="Donald Smith, 1st Baron Strathcona and Mount Royal">Donald Smith</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em;"><a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Minister of Finance and Receiver General</a></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b>In office</b></span><br />January 27, 1887&#160;–&#32;May 22, 1888</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Prime Minister</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. </td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Charles Tupper, Jr.</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1821-07-02</span>)</span>July 2, 1821<br /><a href="/wiki/Amherst,_Nova_Scotia" title="Amherst, Nova Scotia">Amherst</a>, Nova Scotia</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">October 30, 1915<span style="display:none">(1915-10-30)</span> (aged&#160;94)<br /><a href="/wiki/Bexleyheath" title="Bexleyheath">Bexleyheath</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/St._John%27s_Cemetery,_Halifax" class="mw-redirect" title="St. John&#39;s Cemetery, Halifax">St. John's Cemetery, Halifax</a>, Nova Scotia</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Citizenship</th><td class="infobox-data">British subject</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Nationality</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Canadians" title="Canadians">Canadian</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(1867%E2%80%931942)" title="Conservative Party of Canada (1867–1942)">Conservative</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;margin-top:1px;white-space:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Frances_Tupper" title="Frances Tupper">Frances Morse</a></div>

<ul><li>6, including:</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Charles_Hibbert_Tupper" title="Charles Hibbert Tupper">Charles Hibbert Tupper</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/William_Johnston_Tupper" title="William Johnston Tupper">William Johnston Tupper</a></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma mater</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh_Medical_School" title="University of Edinburgh Medical School">University of Edinburgh Medical School</a> (1843)</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Profession</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Physician" title="Physician">Physician</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Awards</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" title="Order of St Michael and St George">Order of St Michael and St George</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Charles Tupper&#39;s signature"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg/128px-Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="28" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg/192px-Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg/256px-Charles_Tupper_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="499" data-file-height="111" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Sir Charles Tupper, 1st Baronet</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">GCMG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Bath" title="Order of the Bath">CB</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a></span>, <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Medicine" title="Doctor of Medicine">M.D.</a><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> (July 2, 1821 – October 30, 1915) was a Canadian <a href="/wiki/Fathers_of_Confederation" title="Fathers of Confederation">Father of Confederation</a> who served as the sixth <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from May 1 to July 8, 1896. As the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Premier of Nova Scotia">premier of Nova Scotia</a> from 1864 to 1867, he led <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a> into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a>. He briefly served as the Canadian prime minister, from seven days after parliament had been dissolved, until he resigned on July 8, 1896, following his party's loss in the <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896 Canadian federal election</a>. He is the only <a href="/wiki/Physician" title="Physician">medical doctor</a> to have ever held the office of prime minister of <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> and his 68-day tenure as prime minister is the shortest in Canadian history.
</p><p>Tupper was born in <a href="/wiki/Amherst,_Nova_Scotia" title="Amherst, Nova Scotia">Amherst, Nova Scotia</a>, to the Rev. Charles Tupper and Miriam Lockhart. He was educated at Horton Academy, Wolfville, Nova Scotia, and studied medicine at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh_Medical_School" title="University of Edinburgh Medical School">University of Edinburgh Medical School</a>, graduating MD in 1843.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> By the age of 22 he had handled 116 <a href="/wiki/Obstetrics" title="Obstetrics">obstetric</a> cases.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He practiced medicine periodically throughout his political career (and served as the first president of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Medical_Association" title="Canadian Medical Association">Canadian Medical Association</a>). He entered Nova Scotian politics in 1855 as a protégé of <a href="/wiki/James_William_Johnston" title="James William Johnston">James William Johnston</a>. During Johnston's tenure as premier of Nova Scotia in 1857–1859 and 1863–1864, Tupper served as <a href="/wiki/Provincial_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Provincial Secretary">provincial secretary</a>. Tupper replaced Johnston as premier in 1864. As premier, he established <a href="/wiki/Public_education" class="mw-redirect" title="Public education">public education</a> in Nova Scotia and expanded Nova Scotia's railway network in order to promote industry.
</p><p>By 1860, Tupper supported a union of all the colonies of <a href="/wiki/British_North_America" title="British North America">British North America</a>. Believing that immediate union of all the colonies was impossible, in 1864, he proposed a <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Union" title="Maritime Union">Maritime Union</a>. However, representatives of the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Canada" title="Province of Canada">Province of Canada</a> asked to be allowed to attend the meeting in <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown" title="Charlottetown">Charlottetown</a> scheduled to discuss Maritime Union in order to present a proposal for a wider union, and the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Conference" title="Charlottetown Conference">Charlottetown Conference</a> thus became the first of the three conferences that secured Canadian Confederation. Tupper also represented Nova Scotia at the other two conferences, the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1864" title="Quebec Conference, 1864">Quebec Conference</a> (1864) and the <a href="/wiki/London_Conference_of_1866" title="London Conference of 1866">London Conference of 1866</a>. In Nova Scotia, Tupper organized a <a href="/wiki/Confederation_Party" title="Confederation Party">Confederation Party</a> to combat the activities of the <a href="/wiki/Anti-Confederation_Party" title="Anti-Confederation Party">Anti-Confederation Party</a> organized by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Howe" title="Joseph Howe">Joseph Howe</a> and successfully led Nova Scotia into Confederation.
</p><p>Following the passage of the <a href="/wiki/British_North_America_Act,_1867" class="mw-redirect" title="British North America Act, 1867">British North America Act</a> in 1867, Tupper resigned as premier of Nova Scotia and began a career in federal politics. He held multiple <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">cabinet</a> positions under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>, including <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Queen%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="President of the Queen&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">President of the Queen's Privy Council for Canada</a> (1870–1872), <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Inland_Revenue_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Inland Revenue (Canada)">Minister of Inland Revenue</a> (1872–1873), <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Customs" title="Minister of Customs">Minister of Customs</a> (1873–1874), <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Public_Works_(Canada)" title="Minister of Public Works (Canada)">Minister of Public Works</a> (1878–1879), and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Railways_and_Canals_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Railways and Canals (Canada)">Minister of Railways and Canals</a> (1879–1884). Initially groomed as Macdonald's successor, Tupper had a falling out with Macdonald, and by the early 1880s, he asked Macdonald to appoint him as <a href="/wiki/Canadian_High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom">Canadian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom</a>. Tupper took up his post in <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a> in 1883, and would remain High Commissioner until 1895, although in 1887–1888, he served as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Minister of Finance</a> without relinquishing the High Commissionership.
</p><p>In 1895, the government of <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_Bowell" title="Mackenzie Bowell">Mackenzie Bowell</a> floundered over the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question" title="Manitoba Schools Question">Manitoba Schools Question</a>; as a result, several leading members of the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservative Party of Canada (historical)">Conservative Party of Canada</a> demanded the return of Tupper to serve as prime minister. Tupper accepted this invitation and returned to Canada, becoming prime minister in May 1896. Just before he was sworn in as prime minister, the 1896 federal election was called, in which his party lost to <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberals</a>. Tupper served as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Opposition</a> from July 1896 until he resigned in February 1901, just months after his <a href="/wiki/1900_Canadian_federal_election" title="1900 Canadian federal election">second defeat at the polls in 1900</a>. He returned to London, England, where he lived until his death in 1915 and was buried back in <a href="/wiki/Halifax,_Nova_Scotia" title="Halifax, Nova Scotia">Halifax</a>, Nova Scotia. He was the last surviving Canadian <a href="/wiki/Fathers_of_Confederation" title="Fathers of Confederation">father of Confederation</a>. In 2016, he was posthumously inducted into the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Medical_Hall_of_Fame" title="Canadian Medical Hall of Fame">Canadian Medical Hall of Fame</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life.2C_1821.E2.80.931855"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life,_1821–1855">Early life, 1821–1855</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life, 1821–1855">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Charles Tupper Jr. was born on July 2, 1821, in <a href="/wiki/Amherst,_Nova_Scotia" title="Amherst, Nova Scotia">Amherst, Nova Scotia</a>, to Charles Tupper Sr. and Miriam Lowe, <span title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names" title="Birth name">née</a></span> Lockhart. He was a descendant of <a href="/wiki/Richard_Warren" title="Richard Warren">Richard Warren</a>, a <i>Mayflower</i> Pilgrim who signed the <a href="/wiki/Mayflower_Compact" title="Mayflower Compact">Mayflower Compact</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Charles Tupper Sr. (1794–1881) was the co-pastor of the local <a href="/wiki/Baptist_church" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptist church">Baptist church</a>. He had been ordained as a Baptist minister in 1817, and was editor of <i>Baptist Magazine</i> 1832–1836. He was an accomplished Biblical scholar, and published <i>Scriptural Baptism</i> (Halifax, Nova Scotia, 1850) and <i>Expository Notes on the Syriac Version of the Scriptures</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Beginning in 1837, at age 16, Tupper attended Horton Academy in <a href="/wiki/Wolfville,_Nova_Scotia" class="mw-redirect" title="Wolfville, Nova Scotia">Wolfville, Nova Scotia</a>, where he learned <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Greek</a>, and some French.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> After graduating in 1839, he spent a short time in <a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a> working as a teacher, then moved to <a href="/wiki/Windsor,_Nova_Scotia" title="Windsor, Nova Scotia">Windsor, Nova Scotia</a>, to study medicine (1839–1840) with Dr. Ebenezer Fitch Harding.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Borrowing money, he then moved to <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a> to study at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh_Medical_School" title="University of Edinburgh Medical School">University of Edinburgh Medical School</a>: he received his MD in 1843.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> During his time in <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a>, Tupper's commitment to his Baptist faith faltered, and he drank <a href="/wiki/Scotch_whisky" title="Scotch whisky">Scotch whisky</a> for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Returning to Nova Scotia in 1846, he broke off an engagement that he had contracted at age 17 with the daughter of a wealthy Halifax merchant, and instead married <a href="/wiki/Frances_Tupper" title="Frances Tupper">Frances Morse</a> (1826–1912), the granddaughter of Colonel Joseph Morse, a founder of <a href="/wiki/Amherst,_Nova_Scotia" title="Amherst, Nova Scotia">Amherst, Nova Scotia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-lady_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lady-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Tuppers had three sons (Orin Stewart, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Hibbert_Tupper" title="Charles Hibbert Tupper">Charles Hibbert</a>, and <a href="/wiki/William_Johnston_Tupper" title="William Johnston Tupper">William Johnston</a>) and three daughters (Emma, Elizabeth Stewart (Lilly), and Sophy Almon).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Tupper children were raised in Frances' <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> denomination and Charles and Frances regularly worshipped in an Anglican church, though on the campaign trail, Tupper often found time to visit Baptist meetinghouses.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper set himself up as a physician in Amherst, Nova Scotia and opened a <a href="/wiki/Pharmacy" title="Pharmacy">drugstore</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_years_in_Nova_Scotia_politics.2C_1855.E2.80.931864"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_in_Nova_Scotia_politics,_1855–1864">Early years in Nova Scotia politics, 1855–1864</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early years in Nova Scotia politics, 1855–1864">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png/220px-Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png/330px-Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png/440px-Charles_Tupper_By_John_Gardiner.png 2x" data-file-width="459" data-file-height="562" /></a><figcaption>Charles Tupper By <a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Gardiner_(painter)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="John Gardiner (painter) (page does not exist)">John Gardiner</a>, <a href="/wiki/Province_House_(Nova_Scotia)" title="Province House (Nova Scotia)">Province House (Nova Scotia)</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The leader of the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Association_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Progressive Conservative Association of Nova Scotia">Conservative Party of Nova Scotia</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_William_Johnston" title="James William Johnston">James William Johnston</a>, a fellow Baptist and family friend of the Tuppers, encouraged Charles Tupper to enter politics.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In 1855 Tupper ran against the prominent <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia_Liberal_Party" title="Nova Scotia Liberal Party">Liberal</a> politician <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Howe" title="Joseph Howe">Joseph Howe</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Cumberland_County,_Nova_Scotia" title="Cumberland County, Nova Scotia">Cumberland County</a> seat in the <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia_House_of_Assembly" title="Nova Scotia House of Assembly">Nova Scotia House of Assembly</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Joseph Howe would be Tupper's political opponent several times in years to come.
</p><p>Although Tupper won his seat, the 1855 election was an overall disaster for the Nova Scotia Conservatives, with the Liberals, led by <a href="/wiki/William_Young_(Nova_Scotia_politician)" title="William Young (Nova Scotia politician)">William Young</a>, winning a large majority.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Young consequently became <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Premier of Nova Scotia">Premier of Nova Scotia</a>.
</p><p>At a <a href="/wiki/Caucus" title="Caucus">caucus</a> meeting in January 1856, Tupper recommended a new direction for the Conservative party: they should begin actively courting Nova Scotia's <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> minority and should eagerly embrace railroad construction.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Having just led his party into a disastrous election campaign, Johnston decided to basically cede control of the party to Tupper, though Johnston remained the party's leader.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> During 1856 Tupper led Conservative attacks on the government, leading Joseph Howe to dub Tupper "the wicked wasp of Cumberland".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In early 1857 Tupper convinced a number of Roman Catholic Liberal members to <a href="/wiki/Cross_the_floor" class="mw-redirect" title="Cross the floor">cross the floor</a> to join the Conservatives, reducing Young's government to the status of a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As a result, Young was forced to resign in February 1857, and the Conservatives formed a government with Johnston as premier.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper became the <a href="/wiki/Provincial_secretary" title="Provincial secretary">provincial secretary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Tupper's first speech to the House of Assembly as provincial secretary, he set forth an ambitious plan of railroad construction.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper had thus embarked on the major theme of his political life: that Nova Scotians (and later Canadians) should downplay their ethnic and religious differences, focusing instead on developing the land's <a href="/wiki/Natural_resources" class="mw-redirect" title="Natural resources">natural resources</a>. He argued that with Nova Scotia's "inexhaustible mines", it could become "a vast manufacturing mart" for the east coast of North America.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He quickly persuaded Johnston to end the General Mining Association's monopoly over Nova Scotia minerals.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1857, Tupper initiated discussions with <a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Canada" title="Province of Canada">Province of Canada</a> concerning an <a href="/wiki/Intercolonial_Railway_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Intercolonial Railway of Canada">intercolonial railway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He traveled to <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a> in 1858 to attempt to secure <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">imperial</a> backing for this project.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> During these discussions, Tupper realized that Canadians were more interested in discussing federal union, while the British (with the <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Earl of Derby</a> in his second term as <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister</a>) were too absorbed in their own immediate interests.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As such, nothing came of the 1858 discussions for an intercolonial railway.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sectarian conflict played a major role in the May 1859 elections, with Catholics largely supporting the Conservatives and <a href="/wiki/Protestants" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestants">Protestants</a> shifting toward the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper barely retained his seat.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives were barely re-elected and lost a <a href="/wiki/Confidence_vote" class="mw-redirect" title="Confidence vote">confidence vote</a> later that year.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Johnston asked the <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Nova_Scotia" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor of Nova Scotia">Governor of Nova Scotia</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_Phipps,_2nd_Marquess_of_Normanby" title="George Phipps, 2nd Marquess of Normanby">Lord Mulgrave</a>, for <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_parliament" title="Dissolution of parliament">dissolution</a>, but Mulgrave refused and invited William Young to form a government.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper was outraged and petitioned the British government, asking them to recall Mulgrave.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For the next three years, Tupper was ferocious in his denunciations of the Liberal government, first Young, and then Joseph Howe, who succeeded Young in 1860.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This came to a head in 1863 when the Liberals introduced legislation to restrict the Nova Scotia <a href="/wiki/Suffrage" title="Suffrage">franchise</a>, a move which Johnston and Tupper successfully blocked.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper continued practicing medicine during this period. He established a successful medical practice in Halifax, rising to become the city medical officer.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In 1863 he was elected president of the Medical Society of Nova Scotia.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the June 1863 election, the Conservatives campaigned on a platform of railroad construction and expanded access to public education.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives won a large majority, taking 44 of the House of Assembly's 55 seats. Johnston resumed his duties as premier and Tupper again became provincial secretary.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As a further sign of the Conservatives' commitment to non-sectarianism, in 1863, after a 20-year hiatus, <a href="/wiki/Dalhousie_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Dalhousie College">Dalhousie College</a> was re-opened as a non-denominational institution of higher learning.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Johnston retired from politics in May 1864 when he was appointed as a judge, and Tupper was chosen as his successor as premier of Nova Scotia.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Premier_of_Nova_Scotia.2C_1864.E2.80.931867"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Premier_of_Nova_Scotia,_1864–1867">Premier of Nova Scotia, 1864–1867</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Premier of Nova Scotia, 1864–1867">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Tupper introduced ambitious education legislation in 1864 creating a system of state-subsidized <a href="/wiki/Common_schools" class="mw-redirect" title="Common schools">common schools</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In 1865 he introduced a bill providing for compulsory local taxation to fund these schools.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Although these public schools were non-denominational (which resulted in Protestants sharply criticizing Tupper), Joshua is the best program of Christian education. However, many Protestants, particularly fellow Baptists, felt that Tupper had sold them out.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> To regain their trust he appointed Baptist educator <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Harding_Rand" title="Theodore Harding Rand">Theodore Harding Rand</a> as Nova Scotia's first <a href="/wiki/Superintendent_of_education" class="mw-redirect" title="Superintendent of education">superintendent of education</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This raised concern among Catholics, led by <a href="/wiki/Thomas-Louis_Connolly" title="Thomas-Louis Connolly">Thomas-Louis Connolly</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Halifax-Yarmouth" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Halifax-Yarmouth">Archbishop of Halifax</a>, who demanded state-funded <a href="/wiki/Catholic_schools" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic schools">Catholic schools</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper reached a compromise with Archbishop Connolly whereby Catholic-run schools could receive public funding, so long as they provided their religious instruction after hours.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Making good on his promise for expanded railroad construction, in 1864 Tupper appointed <a href="/wiki/Sandford_Fleming" title="Sandford Fleming">Sandford Fleming</a> as the chief engineer of the <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia_Railway" title="Nova Scotia Railway">Nova Scotia Railway</a> in order to expand the line from <a href="/wiki/Truro,_Nova_Scotia" title="Truro, Nova Scotia">Truro</a> to <a href="/wiki/Pictou_Landing,_Nova_Scotia" title="Pictou Landing, Nova Scotia">Pictou Landing</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In January 1866 he awarded Fleming a contract to complete the line after local contractors proved too slow.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Though this decision was controversial, it did result in the line's being completed by May 1867.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> A second proposed line, from <a href="/wiki/Annapolis_Royal,_Nova_Scotia" class="mw-redirect" title="Annapolis Royal, Nova Scotia">Annapolis Royal</a> to <a href="/wiki/Windsor,_Nova_Scotia" title="Windsor, Nova Scotia">Windsor</a> initially faltered, but was eventually completed in 1869 by the privately owned <a href="/wiki/Windsor_and_Annapolis_Railway" title="Windsor and Annapolis Railway">Windsor &amp; Annapolis Railway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Tupper.27s_role_in_securing_Canadian_Confederation"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Tupper's_role_in_securing_Canadian_Confederation">Tupper's role in securing Canadian Confederation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Tupper&#039;s role in securing Canadian Confederation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the run-up to the 1859 Nova Scotia election, Tupper had been unwilling to commit to the idea of a union with the other <a href="/wiki/British_North_American" class="mw-redirect" title="British North American">British North American</a> colonies.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> By 1860, however, he had reconsidered his position.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper outlined his changed position in a lecture delivered at <a href="/wiki/Saint_John,_New_Brunswick" title="Saint John, New Brunswick">Saint John, New Brunswick</a>, entitled "The Political Condition of British North America".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The title of the lecture was a homage to <a href="/wiki/John_Lambton,_1st_Earl_of_Durham" title="John Lambton, 1st Earl of Durham">Lord Durham</a>'s 1838 <i><a href="/wiki/Report_on_the_Affairs_of_British_North_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Report on the Affairs of British North America">Report on the Affairs of British North America</a></i> and assessed the condition of British North America in the two decades following Lord Durham's famous report. Although Tupper was interested in the potential economic consequences of a union with the other colonies, the bulk of his lecture addressed the place of British North America within the wider <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Having been convinced by his 1858 trip to London that British politicians were unwilling to pay attention to small colonies such as Nova Scotia, Tupper argued that Nova Scotia and the other <a href="/wiki/Maritimes" class="mw-redirect" title="Maritimes">Maritime</a> colonies "could never hope to occupy a position of influence or importance except in connection with their larger sister Canada".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper therefore proposed to create a "British America", which "stretching from the <a href="/wiki/Atlantic_Ocean" title="Atlantic Ocean">Atlantic</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" title="Pacific Ocean">Pacific</a>, would in a few years exhibit to the world a great and powerful organization, with British Institutions, British sympathies, and British feelings, bound indissolubly to <a href="/wiki/Monarchy_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Monarchy of the United Kingdom">the throne of England</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Charlottetown_Conference.2C_September_1864"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Charlottetown_Conference,_September_1864">Charlottetown Conference, September 1864</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Charlottetown Conference, September 1864">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CharlottetownConference1864.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/CharlottetownConference1864.jpg/200px-CharlottetownConference1864.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/CharlottetownConference1864.jpg/300px-CharlottetownConference1864.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/CharlottetownConference1864.jpg/400px-CharlottetownConference1864.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="553" /></a><figcaption>Tupper and other delegates of the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Conference" title="Charlottetown Conference">Charlottetown Conference</a> on the steps of <a href="/wiki/Government_House_(Prince_Edward_Island)" title="Government House (Prince Edward Island)">Government House</a>, September 1864</figcaption></figure>
<p>With the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a> in 1861, Tupper worried that a victorious <a href="/wiki/Union_(American_Civil_War)" title="Union (American Civil War)">North</a> would turn northward and conquer the British North American provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This caused him to redouble his commitment to union, which he now saw as essential to protecting the British colonies against American aggression.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Since he thought that full union among the British North American colonies would be unachievable for many years, on March 28, 1864, Tupper instead proposed a <a href="/wiki/Maritime_Union" title="Maritime Union">Maritime Union</a> which would unite the Maritime provinces in advance of a projected future union with the Province of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> A conference to discuss the proposed union of Nova Scotia, <a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick" title="New Brunswick">New Brunswick</a> and <a href="/wiki/Prince_Edward_Island" title="Prince Edward Island">Prince Edward Island</a> was scheduled to be held in <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown" title="Charlottetown">Charlottetown</a> in September 1864.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper was pleasantly surprised when the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_the_Province_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Premier of the Province of Canada">Premier of the Province of Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a>, asked to be allowed to attend the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Conference" title="Charlottetown Conference">Charlottetown Conference</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Conference, which was co-chaired by Tupper and <a href="/wiki/New_Brunswick_Premier" class="mw-redirect" title="New Brunswick Premier">New Brunswick Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Leonard_Tilley" title="Samuel Leonard Tilley">Samuel Leonard Tilley</a>, welcomed the Canadian delegation and asked them to join the conference.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The conference proved to be a smashing success, and resulted in an agreement-in-principle to form a union of the four colonies.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Quebec_Conference.2C_October_1864"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Quebec_Conference,_October_1864">Quebec Conference, October 1864</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Quebec Conference, October 1864">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1864" title="Quebec Conference, 1864">Quebec Conference</a> was held on October 10, as a follow-up to the Charlottetown Conference, with <a href="/wiki/Colony_of_Newfoundland" class="mw-redirect" title="Colony of Newfoundland">Newfoundland</a> only attending to observe.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper headed the Nova Scotia delegation to the Quebec Conference.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He supported a legislative union of the colonies (which would mean that there would be only one legislature for the united colonies).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, the <a href="/wiki/French_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="French Canadian">French Canadian</a> delegates to the conference, notably <a href="/wiki/George-%C3%89tienne_Cartier" title="George-Étienne Cartier">George-Étienne Cartier</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hector-Louis_Langevin" title="Hector-Louis Langevin">Hector-Louis Langevin</a>, strongly opposed the idea of a legislative union. Tupper threw his weight behind Macdonald's proposal for a <a href="/wiki/Federalism" title="Federalism">federal</a> union, which would see each colony retain its own legislature, with a central legislature in charge of common interests.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper argued in favour of a strong central government as a second best to a pure legislative union.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He felt, however, that the local legislatures should retain the ability to levy duties on their natural resources.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Concerned that a united legislature would be dominated by the Province of Canada, Tupper pushed for regional representation in the upper house of the confederated colonies (a goal which would be achieved in the makeup of the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate of Canada</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the topic of which level of government would control customs in the union, Tupper ultimately agreed to accept the formula by which the federal government controlled customs in exchange for an annual subsidy of 80 cents a year for each Nova Scotian.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This deal was ultimately not good for Nova Scotia, which had historically received most of its government revenue from customs, and as a result, Nova Scotia entered Confederation with a <a href="/wiki/Government_budget_deficit" class="mw-redirect" title="Government budget deficit">deficit</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:YoungTupper2.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/YoungTupper2.JPG/150px-YoungTupper2.JPG" decoding="async" width="150" height="215" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/YoungTupper2.JPG/225px-YoungTupper2.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/YoungTupper2.JPG/300px-YoungTupper2.JPG 2x" data-file-width="349" data-file-height="500" /></a><figcaption>Tupper in 1865</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath_of_the_Quebec_Conference">Aftermath of the Quebec Conference</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Aftermath of the Quebec Conference">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Although Tupper had given up much at the Quebec Conference, he thought that he would be able to convince Nova Scotians that the deal he negotiated was in some good for Nova Scotia.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He was therefore surprised when the deal he had negotiated at Quebec was roundly criticized by Nova Scotians: the Opposition Leader <a href="/wiki/Adams_George_Archibald" title="Adams George Archibald">Adams George Archibald</a> was the only member of the Liberal caucus to support Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Former premier Joseph Howe now organized an <a href="/wiki/Anti-Confederation_Party" title="Anti-Confederation Party">Anti-Confederation Party</a> and anti-Confederation sentiments were so strong that Tupper decided to postpone a vote of the legislature on the question of Confederation for a full year.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper now organized supporters of Confederation into a <a href="/wiki/Confederation_Party" title="Confederation Party">Confederation Party</a> to push for the union.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In April 1866, Tupper secured a motion of the Nova Scotia legislature in favour of union by promising that he would renegotiate the <a href="/wiki/Seventy-two_Resolutions" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventy-two Resolutions">Seventy-two Resolutions</a> at the upcoming <a href="/wiki/London_Conference_of_1866" title="London Conference of 1866">conference in London</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="London_Conference.2C_1866"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="London_Conference,_1866">London Conference, 1866</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: London Conference, 1866">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Joseph Howe had begun a pamphlet campaign in the UK to turn British public opinion against the proposed union.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Therefore, when Tupper arrived in the UK, he immediately initiated a campaign of pamphlets and letters to the editor designed to refute Howe's assertions.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Tupper did attempt to renegotiate the 72 Resolutions as he had promised, he was ineffective in securing any major changes.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The only major change agreed to at the London Conference arguably did not benefit Nova Scotia – responsibility for the <a href="/wiki/Fisheries" class="mw-redirect" title="Fisheries">fisheries</a>, which was going to be a joint federal-provincial responsibility under the Quebec agreement, became solely a federal concern.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_final_push_for_Confederation">The final push for Confederation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: The final push for Confederation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Following passage of the <a href="/wiki/British_North_America_Act,_1867" class="mw-redirect" title="British North America Act, 1867">British North America Act</a> in the wake of the London Conference, Tupper returned to Nova Scotia to undertake preparations for the union,<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> which came into existence on July 1, 1867, and on July 4, Tupper turned over responsibility for the government of Nova Scotia to <a href="/wiki/Hiram_Blanchard" title="Hiram Blanchard">Hiram Blanchard</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In honour of the role he had played in securing Confederation, Tupper was made a <a href="/wiki/Companion_in_The_Most_Honourable_Order_of_the_Bath" class="mw-redirect" title="Companion in The Most Honourable Order of the Bath">Companion in The Most Honourable Order of the Bath</a> in 1867.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He was now entitled to use the postnomial letters "CB".
</p>
<h2><span id="Career_in_the_Parliament_of_Canada.2C_1867.E2.80.931884"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Career_in_the_Parliament_of_Canada,_1867–1884">Career in the Parliament of Canada, 1867–1884</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Career in the Parliament of Canada, 1867–1884">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Fighting_the_Anti-Confederates.2C_1867.E2.80.931869"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Fighting_the_Anti-Confederates,_1867–1869">Fighting the Anti-Confederates, 1867–1869</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Fighting the Anti-Confederates, 1867–1869">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Canadian_federal_election" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian federal election">first elections</a> for the new <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons of Canada</a> were held in August–September 1867. Tupper ran as a member for the new federal riding of <a href="/wiki/Cumberland_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Cumberland (federal electoral district)">Cumberland</a> and won his seat.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, he was the only pro-Confederation candidate to win a seat from Nova Scotia in the <a href="/wiki/1st_Canadian_Parliament" title="1st Canadian Parliament">1st Canadian Parliament</a>, with Joseph Howe and the Anti-Confederates winning every other seat.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CharlesTupper1870.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/CharlesTupper1870.jpg/220px-CharlesTupper1870.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="259" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/CharlesTupper1870.jpg/330px-CharlesTupper1870.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/CharlesTupper1870.jpg/440px-CharlesTupper1870.jpg 2x" data-file-width="492" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Tupper in April 1870</figcaption></figure>
<p>As an ally of John A. Macdonald and the <a href="/wiki/Liberal-Conservative_Party" title="Liberal-Conservative Party">Liberal-Conservative Party</a>, it was widely believed that Tupper would have a place in the first <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">Cabinet of Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, when Macdonald ran into difficulties in organizing this cabinet, Tupper stepped aside in favour of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Kenny" title="Edward Kenny">Edward Kenny</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Instead, Tupper set up a medical practice in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> and was elected as the first president of the new <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Medical_Association" title="Canadian Medical Association">Canadian Medical Association</a>, a position he held until 1870.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the November 1867 provincial elections in Nova Scotia, the pro-Confederation Hiram Blanchard was defeated by the leader of the Anti-Confederation Party, <a href="/wiki/William_Annand" title="William Annand">William Annand</a>. Given the unpopularity of Confederation within Nova Scotia, Joseph Howe traveled to London in 1868 to attempt to persuade the British government (headed by the Earl of Derby, and then after February 1868 by <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Benjamin Disraeli</a>) to allow Nova Scotia to secede from Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper followed Howe to London where he successfully lobbied British politicians against allowing Nova Scotia to secede.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following his victory in London, Tupper proposed a reconciliation with Howe: in exchange for Howe's agreeing to stop fighting against the union, Tupper and Howe would be allies in the fight to protect Nova Scotia's interests within Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Howe agreed to Tupper's proposal and in January 1869 entered the Canadian cabinet as <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Queen%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="President of the Queen&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">President of the Queen's Privy Council for Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Red_River_Rebellion" title="Red River Rebellion">Red River Rebellion</a> in 1869, Tupper was distressed to find that his daughter Emma's husband was being held hostage by <a href="/wiki/Louis_Riel" title="Louis Riel">Louis Riel</a> and the rebels.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He rushed to the northwest to rescue his son-in-law.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TupperWorking1871.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/TupperWorking1871.jpg/150px-TupperWorking1871.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/TupperWorking1871.jpg/225px-TupperWorking1871.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/TupperWorking1871.jpg/300px-TupperWorking1871.jpg 2x" data-file-width="419" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Tupper in November 1871</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span id="President_of_the_Queen.27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada.2C_1870.E2.80.931872"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="President_of_the_Queen's_Privy_Council_for_Canada,_1870–1872">President of the Queen's Privy Council for Canada, 1870–1872</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: President of the Queen&#039;s Privy Council for Canada, 1870–1872">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When Howe's health declined the next year, Tupper finally entered the <a href="/wiki/1st_Canadian_Ministry" title="1st Canadian Ministry">1st Canadian Ministry</a> by becoming Privy Council president in June 1870.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The next year was dominated by a dispute with the United States regarding US access to the Atlantic <a href="/wiki/Fisheries" class="mw-redirect" title="Fisheries">fisheries</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper thought that the British should restrict American access to these fisheries so that they could negotiate from a position of strength.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> When Prime Minister Macdonald travelled to represent Canada's interests at the negotiations leading up to the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Washington_(1871)" title="Treaty of Washington (1871)">Treaty of Washington (1871)</a>, Tupper served as Macdonald's liaison with the federal cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_of_Inland_Revenue.2C_1872.E2.80.931873"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Inland_Revenue,_1872–1873">Minister of Inland Revenue, 1872–1873</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Minister of Inland Revenue, 1872–1873">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On January 19, 1872, Tupper's service as Privy Council president ended and he became <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Inland_Revenue_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Inland Revenue (Canada)">Minister of Inland Revenue</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper led the Nova Scotia campaign for the Liberal-Conservative party during the <a href="/wiki/1872_Canadian_federal_election" title="1872 Canadian federal election">Canadian federal election of 1872</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His efforts paid off when Nova Scotia returned not a single Anti-Confederate <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(Canada)" title="Member of Parliament (Canada)">Member of Parliament</a> to the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Canadian_Parliament" title="2nd Canadian Parliament">2nd Canadian Parliament</a>, and 20 of Nova Scotia's 21 MPs were Liberal-Conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> (The Liberal-Conservative Party changed its name to the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada_(1867%E2%80%931942)" title="Conservative Party of Canada (1867–1942)">Conservative Party</a> in 1873.)
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_of_Customs.2C_1873.E2.80.931874"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Customs,_1873–1874">Minister of Customs, 1873–1874</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Minister of Customs, 1873–1874">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CharlesTupper1873.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/CharlesTupper1873.jpg/150px-CharlesTupper1873.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/CharlesTupper1873.jpg/225px-CharlesTupper1873.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/CharlesTupper1873.jpg/300px-CharlesTupper1873.jpg 2x" data-file-width="434" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Tupper in August 1873</figcaption></figure>
<p>In February 1873, Tupper was shifted from Inland Revenue to become <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Customs" title="Minister of Customs">Minister of Customs</a>, and in this position he was successful in having <a href="/wiki/Imperial_unit" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial unit">British weights and measures</a> adopted as the uniform standard for the united colonies.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He would not hold this post for long, however, as Macdonald's government was rocked by the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Scandal" title="Pacific Scandal">Pacific Scandal</a> throughout 1873. In November 1873, the 1st Canadian Ministry was forced to resign and was replaced by the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Canadian_Ministry" title="2nd Canadian Ministry">2nd Canadian Ministry</a> headed by <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Mackenzie_(politician)" title="Alexander Mackenzie (politician)">Alexander Mackenzie</a>.
</p>
<h3><span id="Years_in_Opposition.2C_1874.E2.80.931878"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Years_in_Opposition,_1874–1878">Years in Opposition, 1874–1878</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Years in Opposition, 1874–1878">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Tupper had not been involved in the Pacific Scandal, but he nevertheless continued to support Macdonald and his Conservative colleagues both before and after the <a href="/wiki/1874_Canadian_federal_election" title="1874 Canadian federal election">1874 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The 1874 election was disastrous for the Conservatives, and in Nova Scotia, Tupper was one of only two Conservative MPs returned to the <a href="/wiki/3rd_Canadian_Parliament" title="3rd Canadian Parliament">3rd Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though Macdonald stayed on as Conservative leader, Tupper now assumed a more prominent role in the Conservative Party and was widely seen as Macdonald's heir apparent.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He led Conservative attacks on the Mackenzie government throughout the 3rd Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Mackenzie government attempted to negotiate a new <a href="/wiki/Free_trade_agreement" title="Free trade agreement">free trade agreement</a> with the United States to replace the <a href="/wiki/Canadian%E2%80%93American_Reciprocity_Treaty" title="Canadian–American Reciprocity Treaty">Canadian–American Reciprocity Treaty</a> which the U.S. had abrogated in 1864.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> When Mackenzie proved unable to achieve reciprocity, Tupper began shifting toward <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protectionism</a> and became a proponent of the <a href="/wiki/National_Policy" title="National Policy">National Policy</a> which became a part of the Conservative <a href="/wiki/Party_platform" title="Party platform">platform</a> in 1876.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The sincerity of Tupper's conversion to the protectionist cause was doubted at the time, however: according to one apocryphal story, when Tupper came to the 1876 debate on <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Finance Minister</a> <a href="/wiki/Richard_John_Cartwright" title="Richard John Cartwright">Richard John Cartwright</a>'s budget, he was prepared to advocate <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> if Cartwright had announced that the Liberals had shifted their position and were now supporting protectionism.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper was also deeply critical of Mackenzie's approach to railways, arguing that completion of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Railway" title="Canadian Pacific Railway">Canadian Pacific Railway</a>, which would link <a href="/wiki/British_Columbia" title="British Columbia">British Columbia</a> (which entered Confederation in 1871) with the rest of Canada, should be a stronger government priority than it was for Mackenzie.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This position also became an integral part of the Conservative platform.
</p><p>As on previous occasions when he was not in cabinet, Tupper was active in practicing medicine during the 1874–78 stint in Opposition, though he was dedicating less and less of his time to medicine during this period.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper was a councillor of the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_Military_College" title="Oxford Military College">Oxford Military College</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cowley,_Oxfordshire" title="Cowley, Oxfordshire">Cowley</a> and <a href="/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford">Oxford</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oxfordshire" title="Oxfordshire">Oxfordshire</a> from 1876 to 1896.
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_of_Public_Works.2C_1878.E2.80.931879"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Public_Works,_1878–1879">Minister of Public Works, 1878–1879</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Minister of Public Works, 1878–1879">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/1878_Canadian_federal_election" title="1878 Canadian federal election">1878 election</a> Tupper again led the Conservative campaign in Nova Scotia.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The Conservatives under Macdonald won a resounding majority in the election, in the process capturing 16 of Nova Scotia's 21 seats in the <a href="/wiki/4th_Canadian_Parliament" title="4th Canadian Parliament">4th Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the formation of the <a href="/wiki/3rd_Canadian_Ministry" title="3rd Canadian Ministry">3rd Canadian Ministry</a> on October 17, 1878, Tupper became <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Public_Works_(Canada)" title="Minister of Public Works (Canada)">Minister of Public Works</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His top priority was completion of the Canadian Pacific Railway, which he saw as "an Imperial Highway across the Continent of America entirely on British soil".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This marked a shift in Tupper's position: although he had long argued that completion of the railway should be a major government priority, while Tupper was in Opposition, he argued that the railway should be privately constructed; he now argued that the railway ought to be completed as a <a href="/wiki/Public_work" class="mw-redirect" title="Public work">public work</a>, partly because he believed that the private sector could not complete the railroad given the <a href="/wiki/Recession" title="Recession">recession</a> which gripped the country throughout the 1870s.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_of_Railways_and_Canals.2C_1879.E2.80.931884"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_Railways_and_Canals,_1879–1884">Minister of Railways and Canals, 1879–1884</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Minister of Railways and Canals, 1879–1884">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In May 1879, Macdonald decided that completion of the railway was such a priority that he created a new ministry to focus on railways and canals, and Tupper became Canada's first <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Railways_and_Canals_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Railways and Canals (Canada)">Minister of Railways and Canals</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper's motto as Minister of Railways and Canals was "Develop our resources".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He stated "I have always supposed that the great object, in every country, and especially in a new country, was to draw as [many] <a href="/wiki/Capitalism" title="Capitalism">capitalists</a> into it as possible."<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper traveled to London in summer 1879 to attempt to persuade the British government (then headed by <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">the Earl of Beaconsfield</a> in his second term as prime minister) to <a href="/wiki/Guarantee" title="Guarantee">guarantee</a> a <a href="/wiki/Government_bond" title="Government bond">bond</a> sale to be used to construct the railway.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He was not successful, though he did manage to purchase 50,000 tons of steel rails at a bargain price.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper's old friend Sandford Fleming oversaw the railway construction, but his inability to keep costs down led to political controversy, and Tupper was forced to remove Fleming as Chief Engineer in May 1880.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>1879 also saw Tupper made a <a href="/wiki/Knight_Commander_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Commander of the Order of St Michael and St George">Knight Commander of the Order of St Michael and St George</a>, and thus entitled to use the postnominal letters "KCMG".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tupper1881.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Tupper1881.jpg/220px-Tupper1881.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Tupper1881.jpg/330px-Tupper1881.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/11/Tupper1881.jpg 2x" data-file-width="427" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Tupper in September 1881</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1880, <a href="/wiki/George_Stephen,_1st_Baron_Mount_Stephen" title="George Stephen, 1st Baron Mount Stephen">George Stephen</a> approached Tupper on behalf of a <a href="/wiki/Syndicate" title="Syndicate">syndicate</a> and asked to be allowed to take over construction of the railway.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Convinced that Stephen's syndicate was up to the task, Tupper convinced the cabinet to back the plan at a meeting in June 1880 and, together with Macdonald, negotiated a contract with the syndicate in October.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The syndicate successfully created the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Pacific_Railway" title="Canadian Pacific Railway">Canadian Pacific Railway</a> in February 1881 and assumed construction of the railway shortly thereafter.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the following years Tupper was a vocal supporter of the CPR during its competition with the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Railway" title="Grand Trunk Railway">Grand Trunk Railway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In December 1883 he worked out a rescue plan for the CPR after it faced financial difficulties and persuaded his party and Parliament to accept the plan.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to his support for completion of the CPR, Tupper also actively managed the existing railways in the colonies.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after becoming minister in 1879, he forced the <a href="/wiki/Intercolonial_Railway" title="Intercolonial Railway">Intercolonial Railway</a> to lower its freight rates, which had been a major grievance of Maritime business interests.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He then forced the Grand Trunk Railway to sell its <a href="/wiki/Rivi%C3%A8re-du-Loup,_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Rivière-du-Loup, Quebec">Rivière-du-Loup</a> line to the Intercolonial Railway to complete a link between Halifax and the <a href="/wiki/St._Lawrence_Seaway" title="St. Lawrence Seaway">St. Lawrence Seaway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He also refused to give the CPR <a href="/wiki/Running_rights" class="mw-redirect" title="Running rights">running rights</a> over the Intercolonial Railway, though he did convince the CPR to build the Short Line from Halifax to Saint John.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In terms of canals, Tupper's time as Minister of Railways and Canals is notable for large expenditures on widening the <a href="/wiki/Welland_Canal" title="Welland Canal">Welland Canal</a> and deepening the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_Seaway" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Lawrence Seaway">Saint Lawrence Seaway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Deterioration_of_relationship_with_Macdonald_and_appointment_as_High_Commissioner">Deterioration of relationship with Macdonald and appointment as High Commissioner</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Deterioration of relationship with Macdonald and appointment as High Commissioner">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>A rift developed between Tupper and Macdonald in 1879 over Sandford Fleming, whom Tupper supported but whom Macdonald wanted removed as Chief Engineer of the CPR.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This rift was partially healed and Tupper and Macdonald managed to work together during the negotiations with George Stephen's syndicate in 1880, but the men were no longer close, and Tupper no longer seemed to be Macdonald's heir apparent.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> By early 1881 Tupper had determined that he should leave the cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In March 1881 he asked Macdonald to appoint him as Canada's <a href="/wiki/High_Commissioner_(Commonwealth)" class="mw-redirect" title="High Commissioner (Commonwealth)">High Commissioner</a> in London. Macdonald initially refused, and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Tilloch_Galt" title="Alexander Tilloch Galt">Alexander Tilloch Galt</a> retained the High Commissioner's post.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/1882_Canadian_federal_election" title="1882 Canadian federal election">1882 election</a>, Tupper campaigned only in Nova Scotia (he normally campaigned throughout the country): he was again successful, with the Conservatives winning 14 of Nova Scotia's 21 seats in the <a href="/wiki/5th_Canadian_Parliament" title="5th Canadian Parliament">5th Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The 1882 election was personally significant for Tupper because it saw his son, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Hibbert_Tupper" title="Charles Hibbert Tupper">Charles Hibbert Tupper</a>, elected as MP for <a href="/wiki/Pictou_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Pictou (federal electoral district)">Pictou</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Canadian_High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom.2C_1883.E2.80.931895"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Canadian_High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom,_1883–1895">Canadian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom, 1883–1895</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Canadian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom, 1883–1895">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Early_years_as_High_Commissioner.2C_1883.E2.80.931887"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years_as_High_Commissioner,_1883–1887">Early years as High Commissioner, 1883–1887</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Early years as High Commissioner, 1883–1887">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
