<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TupperUniform.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TupperUniform.jpg/220px-TupperUniform.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TupperUniform.jpg/330px-TupperUniform.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/TupperUniform.jpg/440px-TupperUniform.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="611" /></a><figcaption>Tupper circa 1883</figcaption></figure>
<p>Tupper remained committed to leaving Ottawa, however, and in May 1883, he moved to London to become unpaid High Commissioner, though he did not surrender his ministerial position at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, he soon faced criticism that the two posts were incompatible, and in May 1884 he resigned from cabinet and the House of Commons and became full-time paid High Commissioner.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his time as High Commissioner, Tupper vigorously defended Canada's rights.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Although he was not a full <a href="/wiki/Plenipotentiary" title="Plenipotentiary">plenipotentiary</a>, he represented Canada at a <a href="/wiki/Paris" title="Paris">Paris</a> conference in 1883, where he openly disagreed with the British delegation; and in 1884 he was allowed to conduct negotiations for a Canadian commercial treaty with <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper was concerned with promoting <a href="/wiki/Immigration_to_Canada" title="Immigration to Canada">immigration to Canada</a> and made several tours of various countries in <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a> to encourage their citizens to move to Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> A report in 1883 acknowledges the work of Charles Tupper:
</p>
<blockquote><p>As directing emigration from the United Kingdom and also the Continent, his work has been greatly valuable; and especially in reference to the arrangements made by him on the Continent and in Ireland. The High Commissioner for Canada, Sir Charles Tupper, has been aided during the past year by the same Emigration Agents of the Department in the United Kingdom as in 1882, namely, Mr. John Dyke, Liverpool; Mr. Thomas Grahame, Glasgow; Mr. Charles Foy, Belfast; Mr. Thomas Connolly, Dublin, and Mr. J.W. Down, Bristol. On the <a href="/wiki/European_continent" class="mw-redirect" title="European continent">European continent</a>, Dr. Otto Hahn, of Reutlingen, has continued to act as Agent in Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In 1883, Tupper convinced <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">William Ewart Gladstone</a>'s government to exempt Canadian cattle from the general British ban on importing American cattle by demonstrating that Canadian cattle were free of disease.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His other duties as High Commissioner included: putting Canadian exporters in contact with British importers; negotiating loans for the Canadian government and the CPR; helping to organize the <a href="/wiki/Colonial_and_Indian_Exhibition" title="Colonial and Indian Exhibition">Colonial and Indian Exhibition</a> of 1886; arranging for a subsidy for the mail ship from <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, <a href="/wiki/British_Columbia" title="British Columbia">British Columbia</a>, to the <a href="/wiki/Orient" title="Orient">Orient</a>; and lobbying on behalf of a British-Pacific cable along the lines of the <a href="/wiki/Transatlantic_telegraph_cable" title="Transatlantic telegraph cable">transatlantic telegraph cable</a> and for a faster transatlantic <a href="/wiki/Steam_ship" class="mw-redirect" title="Steam ship">steam ship</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper was present at the founding meeting of the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Federation_League" title="Imperial Federation League">Imperial Federation League</a> in July 1884, where he argued against a resolution which said that the only options open to the British Empire were <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Federation" title="Imperial Federation">Imperial Federation</a> or disintegration.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper believed that a form of limited federation was possible and desirable.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Interlude_as_Minister_of_Finance.2C_1887.E2.80.931888"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Interlude_as_Minister_of_Finance,_1887–1888">Interlude as Minister of Finance, 1887–1888</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Interlude as Minister of Finance, 1887–1888">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>1884 saw the election of Liberal <a href="/wiki/William_Stevens_Fielding" title="William Stevens Fielding">William Stevens Fielding</a> as Premier of Nova Scotia after Fielding campaigned on a platform of leading Nova Scotia out of Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As such, throughout 1886, Macdonald begged Tupper to return to Canada to fight the Anti-Confederates.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In January 1887 Tupper returned to Canada to rejoin the 3rd Canadian Ministry as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Minister of Finance of Canada</a>, while retaining his post as High Commissioner.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/1887_Canadian_federal_election" title="1887 Canadian federal election">1887 federal election</a>, Tupper again presented the pro-Confederation argument to the people of Nova Scotia, and again the Conservatives won 14 of Nova Scotia's 21 seats in the <a href="/wiki/6th_Canadian_Parliament" title="6th Canadian Parliament">6th Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his year as finance minister, Tupper retained the government's commitment to protectionism, even extending it to the <a href="/wiki/Iron_and_steel_industry" class="mw-redirect" title="Iron and steel industry">iron and steel industry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> By this time Tupper was convinced that Canada was ready to move on to its second stage of <a href="/wiki/Industrialisation" title="Industrialisation">industrial development</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In part, he held out the prospect of the development of a great iron industry as an inducement to keep Nova Scotia from seceding.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper's unique position of being both Minister of Finance and High Commissioner to London served him well in an emerging crisis in <a href="/wiki/American-Canadian_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="American-Canadian relations">American-Canadian relations</a>: in 1885, the U.S. abrogated the fisheries clause of the Treaty of Washington (1871), and the Canadian government retaliated against American fishermen with a narrow reading of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_1818" title="Treaty of 1818">Treaty of 1818</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Acting as High Commissioner, Tupper pressured the British government (then led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 3rd Marquess of Salisbury">Lord Salisbury</a>) to stand firm in defending Canada's rights.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The result was the appointment of a Joint Commission in 1887, with Tupper serving as one of the three British commissioners to negotiate with the Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Salisbury selected <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a> as one of the British commissioners.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Sparrow_David_Thompson" title="John Sparrow David Thompson">John Thompson</a> served as the British delegation's legal counsel.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> During the negotiations, <a href="/wiki/U.S._Secretary_of_State" class="mw-redirect" title="U.S. Secretary of State">U.S. Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/Thomas_F._Bayard" title="Thomas F. Bayard">Thomas F. Bayard</a> complained that "Mr. Chamberlain has yielded the control of the negotiations over to Charles Tupper, who subjects the questions to the demands of Canadian politics."<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The result of the negotiations was a treaty (the Treaty of Washington of 1888) that made such concessions to Canada that it was ultimately rejected by the <a href="/wiki/American_Senate" class="mw-redirect" title="American Senate">American Senate</a> in February 1888.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, although the treaty was rejected, the Commission had managed to temporarily resolve the dispute.
</p><p>Following the long conclusion of these negotiations, Tupper decided to return to London to become High-Commissioner full-time.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Macdonald tried to persuade Tupper to stay in Ottawa: during the political crisis surrounding the 1885 <a href="/wiki/North-West_Rebellion" title="North-West Rebellion">North-West Rebellion</a>, Macdonald had pledged to nominate Hector-Louis Langevin as his successor; Macdonald now told Tupper that he would break this promise and nominate Tupper as his successor.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper was not convinced, however, and resigned as Minister of Finance on May 23, 1888, and moved back to London.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Later_years_as_High_Commissioner.2C_1888.E2.80.931895"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_years_as_High_Commissioner,_1888–1895">Later years as High Commissioner, 1888–1895</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Later years as High Commissioner, 1888–1895">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Old_Flag!_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle!.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg/150px-The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg/225px-The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg/300px-The_Old_Flag%21_The_Old_Guard_and_the_Old_Principle%21.jpg 2x" data-file-width="474" data-file-height="717" /></a><figcaption>"The Old Flag! The Old Guard and the Old Principle!" Conservative Party election poster, with Charles Tupper and Hugh John Macdonald, during the <a href="/wiki/1891_Canadian_federal_election" title="1891 Canadian federal election">1891 election</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>For Tupper's work on the Joint Commission, Joseph Chamberlain arranged for Tupper to become a <a href="/wiki/Baronetage_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Baronetage of the United Kingdom">baronet of the United Kingdom</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Tupper_Baronetcy" class="mw-redirect" title="Tupper Baronetcy">Tupper Baronetcy</a> was created on September 13, 1888.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1889, tensions were high between the U.S. and Canada when the U.S. banned Canadians from engaging in the <a href="/wiki/Seal_hunt" class="mw-redirect" title="Seal hunt">seal hunt</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Bering_Sea" title="Bering Sea">Bering Sea</a> as part of the ongoing <a href="/wiki/Bering_Sea_Dispute" class="mw-redirect" title="Bering Sea Dispute">Bering Sea Dispute</a> between the U.S. and Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper traveled to <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, to represent Canadian interests during the negotiations and was something of an embarrassment to the British diplomats.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When, in 1890, the provincial secretary of Newfoundland, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bond" title="Robert Bond">Robert Bond</a>, negotiated a fisheries treaty with the U.S. that Tupper felt was not in Canada's interest, Tupper successfully persuaded the British government (then under Lord Salisbury's second term) to reject the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper remained an active politician during his time as High Commissioner, which was controversial because diplomats are traditionally expected to be nonpartisan.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> (Tupper's successor as High Commissioner, <a href="/wiki/Donald_Smith,_1st_Baron_Strathcona_and_Mount_Royal" title="Donald Smith, 1st Baron Strathcona and Mount Royal">Donald Smith</a> would succeed in turning the High Commissioner's office into a nonpartisan office.) As such, Tupper returned to Canada to campaign on behalf of the Conservatives' National Policy during the <a href="/wiki/1891_Canadian_federal_election" title="1891 Canadian federal election">1891 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tupperfamily.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Tupperfamily.jpg/150px-Tupperfamily.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="181" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Tupperfamily.jpg/225px-Tupperfamily.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Tupperfamily.jpg/300px-Tupperfamily.jpg 2x" data-file-width="480" data-file-height="580" /></a><figcaption>Charles Tupper with his son <a href="/wiki/Charles_Hibbert_Tupper" title="Charles Hibbert Tupper">Charles Hibbert Tupper</a> and his grandson, March 1891</figcaption></figure>
<p>Tupper continued to be active in the Imperial Federation League, though after 1887, the League was split over the issue of regular colonial contribution to imperial defense.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As a result, the League was dissolved in 1893, for which some people blamed Tupper.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With respect to the British Empire, Tupper advocated a system of mutual preferential trading. In a series of articles in <i><a href="/wiki/Nineteenth_Century_(periodical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Nineteenth Century (periodical)">Nineteenth Century</a></i> in 1891 and 1892, Tupper denounced the position that Canada should unilaterally reduce its tariff on British goods.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Rather, he argued that any such tariff reduction should only come as part of a wider trade agreement in which tariffs on Canadian goods would also be reduced at the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John A. Macdonald's death in 1891 opened the possibility of Tupper's replacing him as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Prime Minister of Canada">Prime Minister of Canada</a>, but Tupper enjoyed life in London and decided against returning to Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He recommended that his son support <a href="/wiki/John_Sparrow_David_Thompson" title="John Sparrow David Thompson">John Thompson</a>'s prime ministerial bid.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Tupper_becomes_prime_minister.2C_1895.E2.80.931896"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Tupper_becomes_prime_minister,_1895–1896">Tupper becomes prime minister, 1895–1896</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Tupper becomes prime minister, 1895–1896">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>John Thompson died suddenly in office in December 1894. Many observers expected the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General of Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Hamilton-Gordon,_1st_Marquess_of_Aberdeen_and_Temair" title="John Hamilton-Gordon, 1st Marquess of Aberdeen and Temair">Lord Aberdeen</a>, to invite Tupper to return to Canada to become prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, Lord Aberdeen disliked Tupper and instead invited <a href="/wiki/Mackenzie_Bowell" title="Mackenzie Bowell">Mackenzie Bowell</a> to replace Thompson as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TupperCartoon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/TupperCartoon.jpg/220px-TupperCartoon.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="294" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/TupperCartoon.jpg/330px-TupperCartoon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/TupperCartoon.jpg/440px-TupperCartoon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="803" /></a><figcaption>"Sir Charles Tupper et le parlement": <a href="/wiki/Political_cartoon" title="Political cartoon">political cartoon</a> from February 1896</figcaption></figure>
<p>The greatest challenge facing Bowell as prime minister was the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question" title="Manitoba Schools Question">Manitoba Schools Question</a>. The Conservative Party was bitterly divided on how to handle the Manitoba Schools Question, and as a result, on January 4, 1896, seven cabinet ministers resigned, demanding the return of Tupper.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> As a result, Bowell and Aberdeen were forced to invite Tupper to join the <a href="/wiki/6th_Canadian_Ministry" title="6th Canadian Ministry">6th Canadian Ministry</a> and on January 15 Tupper became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Canada" title="Secretary of State for Canada">Secretary of State for Canada</a>, with the understanding that he would become prime minister following the dissolution of the <a href="/wiki/7th_Canadian_Parliament" title="7th Canadian Parliament">7th Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Returning to Canada, Tupper was elected to the 7th Canadian Parliament as member for <a href="/wiki/Cape_Breton_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Cape Breton (federal electoral district)">Cape Breton</a> during a <a href="/wiki/By-election" title="By-election">by-election</a> held on February 4, 1896.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> At this point, Tupper was the <i>de facto</i> prime minister, though legally Bowell was still prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper's position on the Manitoba Schools Act was that French Catholics in Manitoba had been promised the right to separate state-funded French-language Catholic schools in the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Manitoba Act">Manitoba Act</a> of 1870.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Thus, even though he personally opposed French-language Catholic schools in Manitoba, he believed that the government should stand by its promise and therefore oppose <a href="/wiki/Dalton_McCarthy" title="Dalton McCarthy">Dalton McCarthy</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Manitoba Schools Act">Manitoba Schools Act</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He maintained this position even after the Manitoba Schools Act was upheld by the <a href="/wiki/Judicial_Committee_of_the_Privy_Council" title="Judicial Committee of the Privy Council">Judicial Committee of the Privy Council</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1895, the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council ruled that the Canadian federal government could pass remedial legislation to overrule the Manitoba Schools Act (<i>see</i> <a href="/wiki/Disallowance_and_reservation_in_Canada" title="Disallowance and reservation in Canada">Disallowance and reservation</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Therefore, in February 1896 Tupper introduced this remedial legislation in the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> The bill was <a href="/wiki/Filibuster" title="Filibuster">filibustered</a> by a combination of extreme Protestants led by McCarthy and Liberals led by <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Wilfrid Laurier</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This filibuster resulted in Tupper's abandoning the bill and asking for a dissolution.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_May.E2.80.93July_1896"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_May–July_1896">Prime Minister, May–July 1896</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, May–July 1896">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg/220px-CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="302" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg/330px-CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/ff/CharlesandLadyTupper.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Sir Charles and <a href="/wiki/Frances_Tupper" title="Frances Tupper">Lady Tupper</a>, October 1896</figcaption></figure>
<p>Parliament was dissolved on April 24, 1896, and the <a href="/wiki/7th_Canadian_Ministry" title="7th Canadian Ministry">7th Canadian Ministry</a> with Tupper as prime minister was sworn in on May 1<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> making him, with <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a>, one of the only three prime ministers to never sit in Parliament while prime minister. Tupper remains the oldest person ever to become Canadian prime minister, at age 74.
</p><p>Throughout the <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896 election</a> campaign, Tupper argued that the real issue of the election was the future of Canadian industry and insisted that Conservatives needed to unite to defeat the <a href="/wiki/Patrons_of_Industry" title="Patrons of Industry">Patrons of Industry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, the Conservatives were so bitterly divided over the Manitoba Schools Question that wherever he spoke, he was faced with a barrage of criticism, most notably at a two-hour address he gave at <a href="/wiki/Massey_Hall" title="Massey Hall">Massey Hall</a> in Toronto, which was constantly interrupted by the crowd.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wilfrid Laurier, on the other hand, modified the traditional Liberal stance on free trade and embraced aspects of the National Policy.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the end, the Conservatives won the most votes in the 1896 election (48.2 percent of the votes, in comparison to 41.4 percent for the Liberals). However, they captured only about half of the seats in <a href="/wiki/English_Canada" title="English Canada">English Canada</a>, while Laurier's Liberals won a landslide victory in <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, where Tupper's reputation as an ardent <a href="/wiki/Imperialist" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperialist">imperialist</a> was a major handicap.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper had tried and failed to persuade <a href="/wiki/Joseph-Adolphe_Chapleau" title="Joseph-Adolphe Chapleau">Joseph-Adolphe Chapleau</a> to return to active politics as his <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Laurier had clearly won the election on June 24, Tupper initially refused to cede power, insisting that Laurier would be unable to form a government despite the Liberal Party's having won 55 percent of the seats in the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> However, when Tupper attempted to make appointments as prime minister, Lord Aberdeen refused to act on Tupper's advice. Tupper then resigned and Aberdeen invited Laurier to form a government.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper maintained that Lord Aberdeen's actions were <a href="/wiki/Unconstitutional" class="mw-redirect" title="Unconstitutional">unconstitutional</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper's 68 days are the  <a href="/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="List of prime ministers of Canada">shortest term of all prime ministers of Canada</a>. His government never faced a Parliament.
</p><p>His portrait, by <a href="/wiki/Victor_Albert_Long" title="Victor Albert Long">Victor Albert Long</a>, hangs in the Parliament Buildings.
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition.2C_1896.E2.80.931900"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition,_1896–1900">Leader of the Opposition, 1896–1900</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition, 1896–1900">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TupperCrownLife.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/TupperCrownLife.jpg/220px-TupperCrownLife.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/TupperCrownLife.jpg/330px-TupperCrownLife.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/TupperCrownLife.jpg/440px-TupperCrownLife.jpg 2x" data-file-width="609" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Tupper at a meeting of the directors of the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Crown_Life_Insurance_Company&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Crown Life Insurance Company (page does not exist)">Crown Life Insurance Company</a> in Toronto, ca 1900</figcaption></figure>
<p>As Leader of the Opposition during the <a href="/wiki/8th_Canadian_Parliament" title="8th Canadian Parliament">8th Canadian Parliament</a>, Tupper attempted to regain the loyalty of those Conservatives who had deserted the party over the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question" title="Manitoba Schools Question">Manitoba Schools Question</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He played up loyalty to the British Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Tupper strongly supported Canadian participation in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a>, which broke out in 1899, and criticized Laurier for not doing enough to support Britain in the war.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/1900_Canadian_federal_election" title="1900 Canadian federal election">1900 election</a> saw the Conservatives pick up 17 Ontario seats in the <a href="/wiki/9th_Canadian_Parliament" title="9th Canadian Parliament">9th Canadian Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> This was a small consolation, however, Laurier's Liberals won a definitive majority and had a clear mandate for a second term.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Worse for Tupper was the fact he had failed to carry his own seat, losing the Cape Breton seat to Liberal <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Johnston_(Canadian_politician)" title="Alexander Johnston (Canadian politician)">Alexander Johnston</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In November 1900, two weeks after the election, Tupper stepped down as leader of the Conservative Party of Canada and Leader of the Opposition – the caucus chose as his successor fellow Nova Scotian <a href="/wiki/Robert_Laird_Borden" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Laird Borden">Robert Laird Borden</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_years.2C_1901.E2.80.931915"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_years,_1901–1915">Later years, 1901–1915</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Later years, 1901–1915">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Following his defeat in the 1900 election, Tupper and his wife settled with their daughter Emma in <a href="/wiki/Bexleyheath" title="Bexleyheath">Bexleyheath</a> in north-west Kent.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He continued to make frequent trips to Canada to visit his sons <a href="/wiki/Charles_Hibbert_Tupper" title="Charles Hibbert Tupper">Charles Hibbert Tupper</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Johnston_Tupper" title="William Johnston Tupper">William Johnston Tupper</a>, both of whom were Canadian politicians.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax,_NS_(1).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg/250px-Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg/375px-Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ea/Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg/500px-Grave_site_of_Charles_Tupper_in_Halifax%2C_NS_%281%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4032" data-file-height="2268" /></a><figcaption>Grave site of Charles and <a href="/wiki/Frances_Tupper" title="Frances Tupper">Frances Tupper</a> in <a href="/wiki/St._John%27s_Cemetery,_Halifax" class="mw-redirect" title="St. John&#39;s Cemetery, Halifax">St. John's Cemetery</a> in Halifax</figcaption></figure>
<p>On November 9, 1907, Tupper became a member of the <a href="/wiki/Her_Majesty%27s_Most_Honourable_Privy_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Her Majesty&#39;s Most Honourable Privy Council">British Privy Council</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He was also promoted to the rank of <a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George</a>, which entitled him to use the postnominal letters "GCMG".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper remained interested in imperial politics, and particularly with promoting Canada's place within the British Empire. He sat on the executive committee of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire_League" title="British Empire League">British Empire League</a> and advocated closer economic ties between Canada and Britain, while continuing to oppose Imperial Federation and requests for Canada to make a direct contribution to imperial defense costs (though he supported Borden's decision to voluntarily make an emergency contribution of <a href="/wiki/Dreadnoughts" class="mw-redirect" title="Dreadnoughts">dreadnoughts</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> in 1912).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his retirement, Tupper wrote his <a href="/wiki/Memoirs" class="mw-redirect" title="Memoirs">memoirs</a>, entitled <i>Recollections of Sixty Years in Canada</i>, which were published in 1914.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He also gave a series of interviews to journalist W. A. Harkin which formed the basis of a second book published in 1914, entitled <i>Political Reminiscences of the Right Honourable Sir Charles Tupper</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Tupper's wife, <a href="/wiki/Frances_Tupper" title="Frances Tupper">Lady Tupper</a> died in May 1912.<sup id="cite_ref-lady_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lady-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> His eldest son Orin died in April 1915. On October 30, 1915, in Bexleyheath, Tupper died.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> He was the last of the original <a href="/wiki/Fathers_of_Confederation" title="Fathers of Confederation">Fathers of Confederation</a> to die, and had lived the longest life of any Canadian prime minister, at 94 years, four months.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His body was returned to Canada on <a href="/wiki/HMS_Blenheim_(1890)" title="HMS Blenheim (1890)">HMS <i>Blenheim</i></a> (the same vessel that had carried the body of Tupper's colleague, <a href="/wiki/John_Sparrow_David_Thompson" title="John Sparrow David Thompson">John Thompson</a> to Halifax when Thompson died in England in 1894) and was buried in <a href="/wiki/St._John%27s_Cemetery,_Halifax" class="mw-redirect" title="St. John&#39;s Cemetery, Halifax">St. John's Cemetery</a> in Halifax following a <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state funeral</a> with a mile-long procession.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_6-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_recognition">Legacy and recognition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Legacy and recognition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg/170px-Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="187" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg/255px-Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg/340px-Coat_of_arms_of_Charles_Tupper.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="597" data-file-height="657" /></a><figcaption>Coat of arms of Charles Tupper.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Tupper will be most remembered as a <a href="/wiki/Father_of_Confederation" class="mw-redirect" title="Father of Confederation">Father of Confederation</a>, and his long career as a federal cabinet minister, rather than his brief time as Prime Minister. As the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Premier of Nova Scotia">Premier of Nova Scotia</a> from 1864 to 1867, he led <a href="/wiki/Nova_Scotia" title="Nova Scotia">Nova Scotia</a> into <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Confederation" title="Canadian Confederation">Confederation</a> and persuaded <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Howe" title="Joseph Howe">Joseph Howe</a> to join the new federal government, bringing an end to the anti-Confederation movement in Nova Scotia.
</p><p>In their 1999 study of the Canadian Prime Ministers through <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, <a href="/wiki/J.L._Granatstein" class="mw-redirect" title="J.L. Granatstein">J.L. Granatstein</a> and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a> included the results of a survey of Canadian historians ranking the Prime Ministers. Tupper ranked No. 16 out of the 20 up to that time, due to his extremely short tenure in which he was unable to accomplish anything of significance. Historians noted that despite Tupper's elderly age, he showed a determination and spirit during his brief time as Prime Minister that almost beat Laurier in the 1896 election.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Mount_Tupper" title="Mount Tupper">Mount Tupper</a> in the Canadian Rockies and the <a href="/wiki/Sir_Charles_Tupper_Building" title="Sir Charles Tupper Building">Sir Charles Tupper Building</a> in Ottawa are named for him. The Sir Charles Tupper Medical Building is the central building of the <a href="/wiki/Dalhousie_Medical_School" class="mw-redirect" title="Dalhousie Medical School">Dalhousie Medical School</a> in Halifax, Nova Scotia.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Facility_naming">Facility naming</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Tupper&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Facility naming">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Charles_Tupper_Secondary_School" title="Sir Charles Tupper Secondary School">Sir Charles Tupper Secondary School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a>, British Columbia</li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://sct.hrce.ca/">Sir Charles Tupper School in Halifax</a></li>
<li>Sir Charles Tupper Medical Building at the <a href="/wiki/Faculty_of_Medicine,_Dalhousie_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Faculty of Medicine, Dalhousie University">Faculty of Medicine, Dalhousie University</a></li>
<li>Sir Charles Tupper Building in Ottawa</li></ul>
